Weak convergence of stochastic integrals on Skorokhod space in Skorokhod’s \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topologies
Authors: Andreas Søjmark, Fabrice Wunderlich
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We provide criteria for Itô integration to behave continuously with respect to Skorokhod’s \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topologies, when the integrands and integrators converge weakly or in probability. The results are novel in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 setting and unify existing theories in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 case. Beyond sufficient criteria, we present an example of uniformly convergent martingale integrators for which the continuity breaks down. Moreover, we show that, for families of local martingales, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 tightness in fact implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 tightness under a mild localised uniform integrability condition. Finally, we apply our results to study scaling limits of models of anomalous diffusion driven by continuous-time random walks. This yields new results on weak \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 convergence to stochastic integrals against subordinated stable processes. In the case of superdiffusive scaling, an interesting counterexample is obtained.

Introduction

The topic of this paper is the weak convergence of stochastic integrals on Skorokhod space with respect to Skorokhod’s \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topologies. By Skorokhod space, we mean the space of all càdlàg paths \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x:[0,\infty )\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}x:[0,∞)→Rd, for a given dimension d\ge 1d≥1, which we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DRd[0,∞). We let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{J_1}$$\end{document}dJ1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{M_1}$$\end{document}dM1 denote a choice of metrics inducing, respectively, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DRd[0,∞). For their precise definitions and some key properties, see Appendix A. Here we only stress that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DRd[0,∞) is a Polish space when equipped with either of the two topologies.

Consider a sequence of d-dimensional semimartingales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X^n)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Xn)n≥1 on some filtered probability spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega ^n, {\mathcal {F}}^n, {\mathbb {F}}^n, {\mathbb {P}}^n)$$\end{document}(Ωn,Fn,Fn,Pn) and suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n \Rightarrow X$$\end{document}Xn⇒X on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty ),\rho )$$\end{document}(DRd[0,∞),ρ) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho =d_{J_1}$$\end{document}ρ=dJ1 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho =d_{M_1}$$\end{document}ρ=dM1. It is then natural to look for conditions such that X is again a semimartingale and we have a form of weak ‘continuity’ for the operation of Itô integration in the sense that1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Bigl (X^n \,, \,\int _0^\bullet \, H^n_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X^n_s \Bigr ) \; \; \Rightarrow \; \Bigl (X\,,\,\int _0^\bullet \, H_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X_s \Bigr ) \quad \text { on } \quad (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ),\rho ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Xn,∫0∙Hs-ndXsn)⇒(X,∫0∙Hs-dXs)on(DR2d[0,∞),ρ)if also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n \Rightarrow H$$\end{document}Hn⇒H on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty ) , {\tilde{\rho }}\, )$$\end{document}(DRd[0,∞),ρ~) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\rho }}\in \{d_{M_1}, d_{J_1}\}$$\end{document}ρ~∈{dM1,dJ1} and with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn adapted to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}^n$$\end{document}Fn. Here, and throughout, the Itô integrals are defined component-wise.

Remark 1.1

(Convergence together) Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^n$$\end{document}Yn in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^k}[0,\infty ) $$\end{document}DRk[0,∞) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z^n$$\end{document}Zn in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^m}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DRm[0,∞), we say the pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Y^n,Z^n)$$\end{document}(Yn,Zn) converge together (for ρ ρ) if they converge on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{k+m}}[0,\infty ),\rho )$$\end{document}(DRk+m[0,∞),ρ), while they converge jointly (for ρ ρ) if it is on the product space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{k}}[0,\infty ),\rho ) \times (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{m}}[0,\infty ),\rho )$$\end{document}(DRk[0,∞),ρ)×(DRm[0,∞),ρ). Joint convergence can of course be with different metrics \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho , {\tilde{\rho }} \in \{d_{M_1}, d_{J_1}\}$$\end{document}ρ,ρ~∈{dM1,dJ1} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^n$$\end{document}Yn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z^n$$\end{document}Zn.

Working with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 topology, an elegant and effective theory was developed in the seminal papers of Jakubowski, Mémin, & Pagès [24] and Kurtz & Protter [28]. In particular, (1.1) holds under the following two conditions: Firstly, the pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n,X^n)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn) must converge weakly together on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ),d_{J_1})$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,∞),dJ1). Secondly, the semimartingale integrators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn must enjoy some uniform regularity: [24] enforces the P-UT condition (predictable uniform tightness), originally due to Stricker [48], while [28] introduced the UCV condition (uniformly controlled variations). We will not make use of these conditions, but we recall them in Appendix B for reference.

In this paper, we shall instead rely on a new notion of good decompositions for the integrators, and we will focus on different modes of convergence such as joint weak convergence of the integrands and integrators with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho , {\tilde{\rho }} \in \{d_{M_1}, d_{J_1}\}$$\end{document}ρ,ρ~∈{dM1,dJ1}. We find that the notion of good decompositions is easy to work with and can be helpful in pinpointing precisely what is required of each part of the semimartingale decomposition for the integrators.

We stress that convergence results for stochastic integrals continue to find extensive use. Recent examples with Brownian integrators in the limit include scaling limits of Hawkes processes [21], convergence to SPDEs such as random perturbations of the Schrödinger equation [42], and convergence results for Mean Field Games [7, 29] and McKean–Vlasov control problems [30].

Convergence in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology

While the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 topology requires the jump times and jump sizes of convergent paths to approximately match those of the limit, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology is much more flexible. For example, a steeper and steeper continuous ascent may result in a jump, and many smaller jumps of essentially the same sign may morph into one large jump. Still, the topology is strong enough that it is well suited for invariance principles with many key functionals preserving the convergence.

We recall the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology and its key properties in Appendix A. Recent applications in which it plays a decisive role include compactness arguments and limit theorems for queuing problems [12, 36, 39], variants of the Bouchaud trap model [1, 11], dispersing billiards with cusps [25, 34], the supercooled Stefan problem [13, 31, 38], and singular controls [10, 18].

Outside the safe confines of metrizability, Jakubowski [22] introduced another topology on Skorokhod space, called the S topology, which is weaker than the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology (see [3]). Part of the motivation for this comes from close connections to the aforementioned P-UT condition (due to results of [48]), so Jakubowski relies on this when studying stochastic integral convergence on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,1]$$\end{document}DR[0,1] with the S topology in [23]. Two of the main results [23, Thms. 1 and 6] yield the remarkable fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n,X^n)\Rightarrow (H,X)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn)⇒(H,X) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,1],S) \times (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,1],S)$$\end{document}(DR[0,1],S)×(DR[0,1],S) is sufficient for1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^\bullet H^n_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X^n_s \; \Rightarrow \; \int _0^\bullet H_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X_s \quad \text {on} \quad (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,1],S), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0∙Hs-ndXsn⇒∫0∙Hs-dXson(DR[0,1],S),provided the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn satisfy the P-UT condition and provided the interplay of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn respects an additional condition that replaces the convergence together in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 employed by [24, 28]. This latter condition will also play a crucial role in our analysis (for details, see Section 2.2).

Notably, [23] also discusses convergence on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,1]$$\end{document}DR[0,1] for a class of topologies compatible with integration in the sense of the discussion preceding [23, Thm. 4]. Though details are not given, it is noted that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology is compatible with integration in that sense, and so [23, Thm. 4] implies that (1.2) holds on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,1],d_{M_1})$$\end{document}(DR[0,1],dM1) when the integrators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn are weakly convergent on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,1],d_{M_1})$$\end{document}(DR[0,1],dM1) and the relevant conditions are satisfied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn.

As far as we are aware, [23] remains the only work to have studied the general question (1.1) beyond the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 setting. By this, we mean the analysis of general structural conditions such that Itô integrals preserve the convergence of any given families of weakly convergent integrators and integrands on Skorokhod space. There has, however, been related work on certain fast-slow dynamical systems driven by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 convergent processes, with [8] establishing the convergence to corresponding stochastic differential equations in a suitable rough path topology, while convergence in the Skorokhod topologies fails. See also [9, 17] for further results.

Overview of the paper

In Section 2, we give our main results on the weak ‘continuity’ of Itô integration in the sense of (1.1). Section 2.1 introduces two key conditions, including the notion of good decompositions. Section 2.2 then states the central result for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 topologies, together with some simple sufficient criteria. Section 2.3 presents variants of the central result, relaxing the weak convergence conditions on the integrands and considering convergence in probability, and gives a simple-to-use result for local martingale integrators that are continuous in the limit.

In Section 3, we focus on local martingale integrators and their structural properties on Skorokhod space. Section 3.1 constructs what appears to be the first example addressing how (1.1) can fail for sequences of martingale integrators. Next, Section 3.2 presents the remarkable finding that, for local martingales, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 tightness translates to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 tightness under a mild condition of localised uniform integrability. Section 3.3 shows that a slight strengthening of the latter condition yields the preservation of the local martingale property in the limit.

In Section 4, we consider an alternative condition for controlling the interplay between the integrands and integrators. This is presented in Section 4.1 and Section 4.2 then shows how this can be helpful for a general class of pure jump processes.

Section 5 applies our framework to study scaling limits in models of anomalous diffusion expressed via stochastic integrals for continuous-time random walks. Section 5.2 rectifies some issues in the existing literature and, in certain regimes, establishes new results on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 convergence which answer questions raised in [44, 50]. Section 5.3 provides a novel counterexample showing that, in certain other regimes, the convergence can fail in a fundamental way.

Convergence of stochastic integrals

This section presents a series of general results on the weak convergence of stochastic integrals in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topologies. Convergence in probability is also addressed.

Conditions on the integrands and integrators

In [23], Jakubowski introduced a condition for excluding, asymptotically, cases where significant increments of the integrands appear immediately before those of the integrators (a problematic situation where non-vanishing mass of the approximating integrals need not propagate to the limit). This condition relies on the following function adapted from [23, p. 2144].

Definition 2.1

(Consecutive increment function) For every δ >0δ>0 and T>0T>0, we define a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{w}}^T_\delta : \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty ) \times \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty ) \rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}_+$$\end{document}w^δT:DRd[0,∞)×DRd[0,∞)→R+ of the largest consecutive increment within a δ δ period of time on the compact interval [0, T], namely\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {\hat{w}}^T_{\delta }(x,y) := \text {sup}\bigl \{ |x^{(i)}(s)- x^{(i)}(t)| \wedge |y^{(i)}(t)-y^{(i)}(u)| : s< t < u \le s+\delta \le T, \hspace{0.5pt} 1\le i \le d \bigr \} $$\end{document}w^δT(x,y):=sup{|x(i)(s)-x(i)(t)|∧|y(i)(t)-y(i)(u)|:s<t<u≤s+δ≤T,1≤i≤d}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z^{(i)}$$\end{document}z(i) denotes the i-th coordinate of a given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z\in \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}z∈DRd[0,∞). The supremum is of course restricted to positive times s\ge 0s≥0 and, as usual, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\wedge b= \text {min}\{a,b\}$$\end{document}a∧b=min{a,b}.

Definition 2.2

(Asymptotically vanishing consecutive increments) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn be d-dimensional càdlàg processes on given probability spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega ^n, \mathbb{F}^n, {\mathbb {P}}^n)$$\end{document}(Ωn,Fn,Pn). We say that the pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n, X^n)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn)n≥1 have asymptotically vanishing consecutive increments if, for every γ >0γ>0 and T> 0T>0,AVCI\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{\delta \downarrow 0} \; \limsup \limits _{n\rightarrow \infty } \; {\mathbb {P}}^n\bigl ( \, {\hat{w}}_{\delta }^T(H^n, \, X^n) \; > \; \gamma \, \bigr ) = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limδ↓0lim supn→∞Pn(w^δT(Hn,Xn)>γ)=0.

The condition AVCI is a restatement of [23, Eq. (6)] and it will provide the main handle on the interplay between the integrands and integrators. Finally, we also need the integrators to be sufficiently well-behaved. To this end, we shall rely on the following concept.

Definition 2.3

(Good decompositions) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X^n)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Xn)n≥1 be a sequence of semimartingales on a given family of probability spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega ^n, {\mathcal {F}}^n, {\mathbb {F}}^n, {\mathbb {P}}^n)$$\end{document}(Ωn,Fn,Fn,Pn). We say that the sequence has good decompositions if, for the given filtrations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}^n$$\end{document}Fn, there exist decompositions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ X^n = M^n + A^n,\quad M^n \text { local martingales},\quad A^n \text { finite variation processes}, $$\end{document}Xn=Mn+An,Mnlocal martingales,Anfinite variation processes,such that, for every t>0t>0, we haveGD\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{R\rightarrow \infty } \limsup _{n\rightarrow \infty } \; {\mathbb {P}}^n\bigl (\text {TV}_{[0,t]}(A^n)>R\bigr )=0, \quad \limsup _{n\rightarrow \infty } \; {\mathbb {E}}^n\bigl [\, |\Delta M^n_{t \wedge \tau ^n_c}|\, \bigr ] <\infty , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limR→∞lim supn→∞Pn(TV[0,t](An)>R)=0,lim supn→∞En[|ΔMt∧τcnn|]<∞,for all c>0c>0, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ^n_c:= \text {inf}\{s >0: |M^n|^*_s \ge c \}$$\end{document}τcn:=inf{s>0:|Mn|s∗≥c}.

Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {TV}_{[0,t]}(A^n)$$\end{document}TV[0,t](An) denotes the total variation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^n$$\end{document}An on [0, t] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta M^n_t:= M^n_t - M^n_{t-}$$\end{document}ΔMtn:=Mtn-Mt-n denotes the jump of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^n$$\end{document}Mn at time t. Moreover, we have used the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|M^n|^*_t:= \text {sup}_{s\in [0,t]} |M^n_s|$$\end{document}|Mn|t∗:=sups∈[0,t]|Msn|.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n=M^n+A^n$$\end{document}Xn=Mn+An, where the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^n$$\end{document}Mn are local martingales with uniformly bounded jumps and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^n$$\end{document}An are of tight total variation, then (GD) holds. Let us consider a non-trivial example of martingales with diverging jump sizes which are not of tight total variation on compacts, but we can directly verify the criterion on the jumps in (GD).

Example 2.4

Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega , {\mathcal {F}}, {\mathbb {P}})=([0,1], {\mathscr {B}}([0,1]), \text {{Leb}})$$\end{document}(Ω,F,P)=([0,1],B([0,1]),Leb), and define, for t\ge 0t≥0, the processes\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A^n_t(\omega )= & n{{\textbf{1}}}_{[0,\frac{1}{n}]}(\omega ) {{\textbf{1}}}_{[1,\infty )}(t) \\ & + \sum _{k=1}^{n^2} \Bigl [ n^{2}{{\textbf{1}}}_{[0,\frac{1}{n^{k+1}}]}(\omega ) \!-\! \bigl (n^{2} +(-1)^k\bigr ) \!{{\textbf{1}}}_{[\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{n^{k+1}},\frac{1}{n}]}(\omega ) \Bigr ]\! {{\textbf{1}}}_{[1+\frac{k}{n^2},\infty )}(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Atn(ω)=n1[0,1n](ω)1[1,∞)(t)+∑k=1n2[n21[0,1nk+1](ω)-(n2+(-1)k)1[1n-1nk+1,1n](ω)]1[1+kn2,∞)(t).There is a single large jump up at time 1 with small probability, and then a growing number of larger jumps up or down with smaller and smaller probability between times 1 and 2. The natural right-continuous filtration is obtained from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}^n_0=\{\emptyset , \Omega \}$$\end{document}F0n={∅,Ω}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}^n_1= \{\emptyset , \Omega ,[0,1/n], (1/n,1]\}$$\end{document}F1n={∅,Ω,[0,1/n],(1/n,1]}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}^n_{1+k/n^2}= \sigma (\{\emptyset , \Omega ,[0,1/n], [0,1/n^{\ell +1}], [1/n-1/n^{\ell +1}, 1/n] : \ell =1,...,k\})$$\end{document}F1+k/n2n=σ({∅,Ω,[0,1/n],[0,1/nℓ+1],[1/n-1/nℓ+1,1/n]:ℓ=1,...,k}), for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1,...,n^2$$\end{document}k=1,...,n2. As in the Doob–Meyer decomposition, we can see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^n:=A^n - B^n$$\end{document}Mn:=An-Bn is a martingale, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^n$$\end{document}Bn is the simple right-continuous process generated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^n_0\equiv 0$$\end{document}B0n≡0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^n_1\equiv 1$$\end{document}B1n≡1, and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ B^n_{1+\frac{k}{n^2}} = 1+ \frac{1}{n} \quad \text {if } k \text { is odd},\quad \text {or }\quad B^n_{1+\frac{k}{n^2}} = 1 \quad \text {if } k \text { is even} ,\quad \text {for}\quad k=1,...,n^2. $$\end{document}B1+kn2n=1+1nifkis odd,orB1+kn2n=1ifkis even,fork=1,...,n2.While \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^n$$\end{document}Mn converges ucp to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto {{\textbf{1}}}_{[1,\infty )}(t)$$\end{document}t↦1[1,∞)(t), neither \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^n$$\end{document}An nor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^n$$\end{document}Bn are of tight total variation on compacts, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {TV}_{[0,2]}(M^n)=\text {TV}_{[0,2]}(B^n)= 1+ n$$\end{document}TV[0,2](Mn)=TV[0,2](Bn)=1+n on the event (1/n, 1]. Moreover, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {E}}[\text {sup}_{0\le s\le 2} |\Delta M^n_s|]\ge {\mathbb {E}}[|\Delta M^n_{1+k/n^2}|]\ge n \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}E[sup0≤s≤2|ΔMsn|]≥E[|ΔM1+k/n2n|]≥n→∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞. Nevertheless, given c>0c>0, one readily sees that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {E}}[ |\Delta M^n_{t \wedge \tau ^n_c}|] \le 2$$\end{document}E[|ΔMt∧τcnn|]≤2 for all n\ge cn≥c and t\ge 1t≥1, due to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\Delta M^n_{t\wedge \tau ^n_c}|=|\Delta M^n_{1}|=n$$\end{document}|ΔMt∧τcnn|=|ΔM1n|=n on the event [0, 1/n], so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^n$$\end{document}Mn satisfies the jump condition in (GD).

We shall write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n \Rightarrow _{f.d.d.} X$$\end{document}Xn⇒f.d.d.X on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \subseteq [0,\infty )$$\end{document}D⊆[0,∞) if, for all m\ge 1m≥1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_1,...,t_m \in D$$\end{document}t1,...,tm∈D, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {\mathbb {P}}^n \circ (X^n_{t_1},...,X^n_{t_m})^{-1} \; \Rightarrow \; {\mathbb {P}}\circ (X_{t_1},...,X_{t_m})^{-1}\, .$$\end{document}Pn∘(X1n,...,Xmn)-1⇒P∘(Xt1,...,Xtm)-1.If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n \Rightarrow X$$\end{document}Xn⇒X for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1, then this holds on the set D of almost sure continuity points of X. We can then recall the following result from [24], modified to rely on good decompositions.

Proposition 2.5

(Preservation of the semimartingale property) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n,X^n$$\end{document}Hn,Xn be adapted càdlàg processes defined on filtered probability spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega ^n, {\mathcal {F}}^n, {\mathbb {F}}^n, {\mathbb {P}}^n)$$\end{document}(Ωn,Fn,Fn,Pn). Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(|X^n|^*_t)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(|Xn|t∗)n≥1 is tight, for all t>0t>0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n,X^n) \Rightarrow _{f.d.d.} (H,X)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn)⇒f.d.d.(H,X) on a dense subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D\subseteq [0,\infty )$$\end{document}D⊆[0,∞), for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H,X \in \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty ) $$\end{document}H,X∈DRd[0,∞). If the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn admit good decompositions (GD), then X is a semimartingale for the natural filtration generated by the pair (H, X).

While this is often left implicit in our writing, it is of course crucial that (GD) holds with respect to the given filtrations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}^n$$\end{document}Fn for which the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn are adapted.

Weak convergence of stochastic integrals on Skorokhod space

For a given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}Rd-valued semimartingale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=(X^{(1)},...,X^{(d)})$$\end{document}X=(X(1),...,X(d)) and a given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}Rd-valued integrand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=(H^{(1)},...,H^{(d)})$$\end{document}H=(H(1),...,H(d)), we define the stochastic integral of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_-$$\end{document}H- against X componentwise, i.e.2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^\bullet \, H_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X_s \; := \; \Bigl ( \, \int _0^\bullet \, H^{(1)}_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X_s^{(1)}, \, ... \,, \, \int _0^\bullet \, H^{(d)}_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X_s^{(d)} \, \Bigr ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0∙Hs-dXs:=(∫0∙Hs-(1)dXs(1),...,∫0∙Hs-(d)dXs(d)).We can now state our first result on the weak convergence properties of stochastic integrals.

Theorem 2.6

(Weak ‘continuity’ of Itô integrals) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega ^n, {\mathcal {F}}^n, {\mathbb {F}}^n, {\mathbb {P}}^n)$$\end{document}(Ωn,Fn,Fn,Pn) be a family of filtered probability spaces supporting a sequence of semimartingales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X^n)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Xn)n≥1 with good decompositions (GD). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Hn)n≥1 be any given sequence of adapted càdlàg processes for the same filtered probability spaces such that (i) there is joint weak convergence2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (H^n,X^n) \; \Rightarrow \; (H,X) \quad \text { on } \quad (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )\, , \, {\tilde{\rho }}\, )\times (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )\, , \,\rho ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn)⇒(H,X)on(DRd[0,∞),ρ~)×(DRd[0,∞),ρ)with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ,{\tilde{\rho }} \in \{ d_{M_1}, d_{J_1}\}$$\end{document}ρ,ρ~∈{dM1,dJ1}, for some càdlàg limits H and X, and (ii) the pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n, X^n)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn) satisfy (AVCI). Then, X is a semimartingale in the filtration generated by the pair (H, X) and2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Bigl ( X^n, \, \int _0^\bullet \, H^n_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X_s^n \Bigr ) \; \; \Rightarrow \; \Bigl ( X, \, \int _0^\bullet \, H_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X_s \Bigr ) \quad \text {on} \quad (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ), \, \rho ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Xn,∫0∙Hs-ndXsn)⇒(X,∫0∙Hs-dXs)on(DR2d[0,∞),ρ).If, in addition, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n,X^n) \, \Rightarrow \, (H,X)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn)⇒(H,X) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ),\rho )$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,∞),ρ), then it furthermore holds that2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Bigl ( H^n, \, X^n, \, \int _0^\bullet \, H^n_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X_s^n \Bigr ) \; \; \Rightarrow \; \Bigl ( H, \, X, \, \int _0^\bullet \, H_{s-} \, \textrm{d}X_s \Bigr ) \quad \text {on} \quad (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{3d}}[0,\infty ), \, \rho ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn,∫0∙Hs-ndXsn)⇒(H,X,∫0∙Hs-dXs)on(DR3d[0,∞),ρ).

Remark 2.7

Naturally, all statements also hold with [0,∞ )[0,∞) replaced by [0, T], for T>0T>0.

While (AVCI) seems difficult to work with in general, our next result singles out two simple sufficient criteria. These provide intuition, and may be all that is needed in many applications. The first criterion makes a precise connection to the conditions of [24, 28] (and [20, 27]) in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 setting. Concerning the second criterion, it was noted in [23, Rmk. 4] that if the integrands or integrators form a tight sequence for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 topology with all limit points supported on the space of continuous paths, then (AVCI) is satisfied. As a natural generalisation of this, we make the useful observation that one only needs to rule out common jumps in the limit.

Proposition 2.8

(Sufficient conditions for AVCI) In the setting of Theorem 2.6, the condition (AVCI) is satisfied if one of the following two criteria holds: (i)The pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n, X^n)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn) converge together to (H, X) weakly in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 topology, meaning that (2.2) holds on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ), \, d_{J_1})$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,∞),dJ1).(ii)The limiting processes H and X almost surely have no common discontinuities, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \text {Disc}(H) \; \cap \; \text {Disc}(X) \; = \; \emptyset \quad \text {almost surely}. $$\end{document}Disc(H)∩Disc(X)=∅almost surely.

(i)The pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n, X^n)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn) converge together to (H, X) weakly in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 topology, meaning that (2.2) holds on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ), \, d_{J_1})$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,∞),dJ1).

The pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n, X^n)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn) converge together to (H, X) weakly in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 topology, meaning that (2.2) holds on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ), \, d_{J_1})$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,∞),dJ1).

(ii)The limiting processes H and X almost surely have no common discontinuities, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \text {Disc}(H) \; \cap \; \text {Disc}(X) \; = \; \emptyset \quad \text {almost surely}. $$\end{document}Disc(H)∩Disc(X)=∅almost surely.

The limiting processes H and X almost surely have no common discontinuities, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \text {Disc}(H) \; \cap \; \text {Disc}(X) \; = \; \emptyset \quad \text {almost surely}. $$\end{document}Disc(H)∩Disc(X)=∅almost surely.

Remark 2.9

(Weak and strong \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology) Whitt’s monograph [51] defines a ‘strong’ and ‘weak’ \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology for d\ge 2d≥2. The standard (or ‘strong’) metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{M_1}$$\end{document}dM1 from Definition A.2 induces the ‘strong’ topology. The ‘weak’ version is simply the product topology on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{d}}[0,\infty ) \cong (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,\infty ))^{ d} $$\end{document}DRd[0,∞)≅(DR[0,∞))d when each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DR[0,∞) is endowed with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology. All our results also hold if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{M_1}$$\end{document}dM1 is taken to be a metric inducing the ‘weak’ \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology. The proofs remain identical, except for the arguments to control (T2) in the proof of Theorem 2.6 and its later variant, Theorem 4.1 (see Section 6.2.2). With the d-fold product topology, one instead relies on multiple applications of Proposition 2.15 in dimension one and otherwise proceeds analogously.

In applications, tightness is typically much easier to verify individually for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn. This is sufficient for obtaining joint weak convergence of the pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n,X^n)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn) on the product space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty ) \times \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DRd[0,∞)×DRd[0,∞). By contrast, convergence together (i.e., for the ‘strong’ topology) requires tightness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DR2d[0,∞) which is a substantially more restrictive property.

Remark 2.10

(Matrices and dot product integrals) If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{H}}$$\end{document}H is a (k × m)(k×m)-matrix and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{X}}$$\end{document}X is an (m × d)(m×d)-matrix, we can define a matrix stochastic integral \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^\bullet \, \!{\boldsymbol{H}}_{s-} \textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{X}}_s$$\end{document}∫0∙Hs-dXs as the (k × d)(k×d)-matrix whose components are the ‘dot product’ stochastic integrals\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Bigl ( \, \int _0^\bullet \, {\boldsymbol{H}}_{s-} \textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{X}}_s\, \Bigr )_{j,\ell } \; := \; \int _0^\bullet \,{\boldsymbol{H}}^{(\text {row}\,j)}_{s-} \cdot \textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(\text {col}\,\ell )}_s \; := \; \sum _{i=1}^m \int _0^\bullet \, H^{(j,i)}_{s-}\, \textrm{d}X^{(i,\ell )}_s. $$\end{document}(∫0∙Hs-dXs)j,ℓ:=∫0∙Hs-(rowj)·dXs(colℓ):=∑i=1m∫0∙Hs-(j,i)dXs(i,ℓ).By Proposition A.3, linearly combining components is a continuous operation for convergence together on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{kd}}[0,\infty ), \rho )$$\end{document}(DRkd[0,∞),ρ). Thus, the continuous mapping theorem and Theorem 2.6 give weak convergence of matrix stochastic integrals on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{k\times d}}[0,\infty ), \rho )$$\end{document}(DRk×d[0,∞),ρ), by identifying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^{k \times d}$$\end{document}Rk×d with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^{kd}$$\end{document}Rkd, if we assume (AVCI) holds for each sequence of pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\boldsymbol{H}}^{n,(\text {row}\,j)},{\boldsymbol{X}}^{n,(\text {col}\,\ell )})_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Hn,(rowj),Xn,(colℓ))n≥1.

Convergence in probability and relaxed conditions on integrands

In view of Theorem 2.6, it is not surprising that, if, in place of weak convergence, we require the integrands and integrators to converge in probability on Skorokhod space, then this mode of convergence carries over to the stochastic integrals. The next result makes this precise.

Theorem 2.11

(Functional convergence in probability) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X^n)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Xn)n≥1 be a sequence of d-dimensional semimartingales with good decompositions (GD) on a common filtered probability space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega , {\mathcal {F}}, {\mathbb {F}}, {\mathbb {P}})$$\end{document}(Ω,F,F,P). Consider a sequence of adapted càdlàg processes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Hn)n≥1 on the same filtered probability space such that2.5for every γ >0γ>0, for some adapted càdlàg processes H and X, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\rho }},\rho \in \{d_{M_1}, d_{J_1}\}$$\end{document}ρ~,ρ∈{dM1,dJ1}. Furthermore, suppose the pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n, X^n)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn) satisfy (AVCI). Then, X is a semimartingale in the natural filtration generated by the pair (H, X) and, on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DR2d[0,∞), we have2.6for all γ >0γ>0. If, additionally, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ((H^n, X^n), (H, X))$$\end{document}ρ((Hn,Xn),(H,X)) tends to zero in probability, then there is also convergence together with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn in (2.6) on the strong space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{3d}}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DR3d[0,∞).

Convergence in probability is also considered in [23, 24, 28]. As in those works, the proof amounts only to the slightest of modifications to the proof of weak convergence in Theorem 2.6.

The results of [23] for the S topology on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,1]$$\end{document}DR[0,1] show that we can aim for weaker assumptions on the integrands. The next result adapts this to our setting. To this end, we shall need the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^T_\delta $$\end{document}NδT that returns the number of δ δ-increments of a path up to time T, namely2.7for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha :[0,T] \rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}α:[0,T]→Rd. Moreover, we will write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Disc}_{\mathbb {P}}(H,X):=\{s>0: {\mathbb {P}}(\Delta (H,X)\ne 0)>0\}$$\end{document}DiscP(H,X):={s>0:P(Δ(H,X)≠0)>0}, and we recall that this defines a countable set of times for càdlàg processes H and X.
