Now, note that on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\sigma ^n_{k+1}>q_2\}$$\end{document}{σk+1n>q2} it holds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi ^n(t_i^n)\in \{0, \sigma ^n_1,..., \sigma ^n_{k-J}\}$$\end{document}Ξn(tin)∈{0,σ1n,...,σk-Jn} for all i=1,...,ℓ i=1,...,ℓ since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_i^n \le q_2 < \sigma ^n_{k+1}$$\end{document}tin≤q2<σk+1n. We can write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\big \{ g_{n,i}(t^n_{i},X^n_{\Xi ^n(t^n_{i})}) \in A_i\big \} \; = \; \overset{k}{\bigcup \limits _{\ell =0}}\; \; \Big ( \, \big \{ \, \sigma _\ell ^n \le t^n_i < \sigma _{\ell +1}^n \, \big \} \; \cap \; \big \{ \, g_{n,i}(t^n_i, X^n_{\sigma ^n_{\ell -J}}) \in A_i \, \big \} \, \Big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}{gn,i(tin,XΞn(tin)n)∈Ai}=⋃ℓ=0k({σℓn≤tin<σℓ+1n}∩{gn,i(tin,Xσℓ-Jnn)∈Ai})on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\sigma ^n_{k+1}>q_2\}$$\end{document}{σk+1n>q2}, where we have defined \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^n_{j}:=0$$\end{document}σjn:=0 for all jłe 0j≤0. The involved sets, however, are measurable sets with respect to the σ σ-algebra\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma \big ( \, X^n_{0^{-}},\, \sigma ^n_1,...,\sigma ^n_{k+1}, \, \xi ^n_{k-J+j}\, \big )$$\end{document}σ(X-n,σ1n,...,σk+1n,ξk-J+jn)while the process \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n_{\sigma ^n_{k+1} \, + \, \bullet } - X^n_{\sigma ^n_k}$$\end{document}Xσk+1n+∙n-Xσknn is measurable with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ( \, \Delta \sigma ^n_{k+1+j}, \, \xi ^n_{k-J+j} : j\ge 1)$$\end{document}σ(Δσk+1+jn,ξk-J+jn:j≥1). Hence, we obtain the desired independence. □ □

Applications driven by continuous-time random walks

The notion of continuous-time random walks (CTRWs) was introduced by Montroll & Weiss [37] for the mathematical study of anomalous diffusion. It is a type of renewal-reward process whose precise definition is given in Section 5.1 below. Related to the present work, [44] studies functional weak convergence of stochastic integrals driven by CTRWs. This was motivated by the analysis of damped harmonic oscillators subject to stable Lévy noise in [47]. Indeed, stochastic integrals against stable processes are often considered for physical models with heavy tailed behaviour due to stable limit theorems [16] (see also [26] for related work on Langevin equations with stable Lévy noise). With a view towards the analysis of more general anomalous diffusion models, [50, Ch. 6] highlights the limitations of what is known about weak continuity results on Skorokhod space for stochastic integrals against general CTRW integrators (see in particular [50, Rem. 6.4]). We address this in the next subsections.

Continuous-time random walk: definitions and scaling limits

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \theta _k : -\infty< k < \infty \}$$\end{document}{θk:-∞<k<∞} be a sequence of i.i.d. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}Rd-valued random variables on a common probability space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega , {\mathcal {F}}, {\mathbb {P}})$$\end{document}(Ω,F,P) that are in the normal domain of attraction of a non-degenerate α α-stable random variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\theta }}$$\end{document}θ~ with 0< α < 20<α<2 (for α =2α=2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\tilde{\theta }}}$$\end{document}θ~ is non-degenerate Gaussian). Given non-negative constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0,c_1,...\ge 0$$\end{document}c0,c1,...≥0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{j=0}^\infty c_j^\rho < \infty $$\end{document}∑j=0∞cjρ<∞ for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\rho <\alpha $$\end{document}0<ρ<α, we define5.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \zeta _i \; := \; \sum _{j=0}^\infty \; c_j \theta _{i-j}, \qquad i \ge 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ζi:=∑j=0∞cjθi-j,i≥1.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1<\alpha \le 2$$\end{document}1<α≤2, assume further that either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j=0$$\end{document}cj=0 for all but finitely many j\ge 0j≥0 or the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(c_j)_{j\ge 0}$$\end{document}(cj)j≥0 is monotone and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{j=0}^\infty c_j^\rho <\infty $$\end{document}∑j=0∞cjρ<∞ for some ρ <1ρ<1. Now let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1, J_2,...$$\end{document}J1,J2,... be positive i.i.d. random variables in the normal domain of attraction of a β β-stable random variable with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in (0,1)$$\end{document}β∈(0,1), defined on the same probability space as the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _k$$\end{document}θk.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(k):= J_1+\hdots +J_k$$\end{document}L(k):=J1+⋯+Jk give the time of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k^{\text {th}}$$\end{document}kth jump (with L(0)≡ 0L(0)≡0). Then is the number of jumps by time t, and we refer to each process5.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X^n_t \; := \; \frac{1}{n^{\frac{\beta }{\alpha }}} \; \sum _{k=1}^{N(nt)} \, \zeta _k \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xtn:=1nβα∑k=1N(nt)ζkas a (rescaled) correlated CTRW, in view of the linear correlation structure (5.1). Taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0=1$$\end{document}c0=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j=0$$\end{document}cj=0 for j\ge 1j≥1 yields a standard (uncorrelated) CTRW. The CTRWs in (5.2) are further said to be uncoupled if the sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(J_i)_{i\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Ji)i≥1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\zeta _k)_{k\ge 1}$$\end{document}(ζk)k≥1 are independent. In this setting, it is shown in [32] that, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\alpha \le 2$$\end{document}0<α≤2, there is weak convergence5.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \; X^n \; \; {\mathop {{\Rightarrow } }\limits ^{{M_1}}} \; \; X=\Bigl (\,\sum _{j=0}^\infty c_j\Bigr )Z_{D^{-1}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xn⇒M1X=(∑j=0∞cj)ZD-1,where Z is an α α-stable Lévy process (0<α <20<α<2) or a Brownian motion (α =2α=2) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_1\sim {{\tilde{\theta }}}$$\end{document}Z1∼θ~, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ D^{-1}(t) := \text {inf} \{ s\ge 0 \, : \, D(s) > t \}$$\end{document}D-1(t):=inf{s≥0:D(s)>t} is the generalised inverse of a β β-stable subordinator D.

In the uncorrelated case (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0>0$$\end{document}c0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j=0$$\end{document}cj=0 for j\ge 1j≥1), this was first studied by [4, 33], and [19] later showed that (5.3) in fact holds in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 topology. As observed in [32], (5.3) cannot be improved to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 convergence when not only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0>0$$\end{document}c0>0 but also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j>0$$\end{document}cj>0 for some j\ge 1j≥1.

Weak continuity results for continuous-time random walks

Consider a sequence of uncorrelated, uncoupled CTRWs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn on the real line. Assuming also the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _k$$\end{document}θk are symmetric and α α-stable, the first main result of [44] shows that, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1,2]$$\end{document}α∈(1,2], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^\bullet f(s)\textrm{d}X^n_s$$\end{document}∫0∙f(s)dXsn converges weakly to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^\bullet f(s)\textrm{d}X_s$$\end{document}∫0∙f(s)dXs on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,\infty ),d_{M_1})$$\end{document}(DR[0,∞),dM1), where f is a given bounded continuous function [44, Thm. 4.15]. The second main result generalises this statement to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,2]$$\end{document}α∈(0,2] and the general assumptions of Section 5.1 [44, Thm. 4.20]. Unfortunately, a crucial step [44, Eq. (62)] in the proof of the latter can be seen to be false for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in [1,2]$$\end{document}α∈[1,2] and the justification is lacking for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,1)$$\end{document}α∈(0,1). Nevertheless, we shall see that a much more general statement is indeed true.

In the special case α =2α=2 (with centered \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _k$$\end{document}θk), where X is a subordinated Brownian motion, [50, Thm. 6.13] confirms that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^\bullet H^n_{s-}\textrm{d}X^n_s$$\end{document}∫0∙Hs-ndXsn converges weakly to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^\bullet H_s \textrm{d}X_s$$\end{document}∫0∙HsdXs on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}}[0,\infty ),d_{J_1})$$\end{document}(DR[0,∞),dJ1) whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n,X^n) \Rightarrow (H,X)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn)⇒(H,X) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^2}[0,\infty ),d_{J_1})$$\end{document}(DR2[0,∞),dJ1). The possible extension to general integrands and general CTRWs, including the coupled case, is discussed in [44, 50].

A coupled CTRW \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn refers to the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\zeta _k,J_k)_{k\ge 1}$$\end{document}(ζk,Jk)k≥1 is an i.i.d. sequence, but, for each k\ge 1k≥1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _k$$\end{document}ζk and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_k$$\end{document}Jk may be dependent. In the uncorrelated setting, [19] has shown that5.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \; X^n \; \; {\mathop {{\Rightarrow } }\limits ^{{J_1}}} \; \; \bigl ((Z^-)_{D^{-1}}\bigr )^+, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xn⇒J1((Z-)D-1)+,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x^-(t):=x(t-)$$\end{document}x-(t):=x(t-) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x^+(t):=x(t+)$$\end{document}x+(t):=x(t+). If the CTRWs are in fact uncoupled, then Z and D are independent Lévy processes, so they have no common jumps, a.s., and hence the limit simplifies to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{D^{-1}}$$\end{document}ZD-1 in agreement with (5.3). We can make the following key observation, the proof of which we have deferred to [46] for brevity.

Theorem 5.1

(Good decompositions) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn be uncorrelated, possibly coupled, CTRWs, as given by (5.2) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0>0$$\end{document}c0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j=0$$\end{document}cj=0 for all j\ge 1j≥1. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X^n)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Xn)n≥1 has good decompositions (GD) for its natural filtration. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,1)$$\end{document}α∈(0,1), this holds also for correlated CTRWs.

With this result, our framework applies. In the uncorrelated case, the CTRWs are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 convergent (5.4), so we can in particular conclude that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X^n,\int _0^\bullet H^n_{s-}\textrm{d}X^n_s)$$\end{document}(Xn,∫0∙Hs-ndXsn) converges weakly to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X,\int _0^\bullet H_{s-} \textrm{d}X_s)$$\end{document}(X,∫0∙Hs-dXs) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ),d_{J_1})$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,∞),dJ1) whenever the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn satisfy (F1)–(F3) and (AVCI), by Proposition 2.12. This of course holds if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n,X^n) \Rightarrow (H,X)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn)⇒(H,X) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ),d_{J_1})$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,∞),dJ1), by Proposition 2.8. Thus, we refine and vastly generalise the results from [44, 50] discussed above.

In the correlated case with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,1)$$\end{document}α∈(0,1), (5.3) holds for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology only. The second part of Theorem 5.1 then tells us that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X^n,\int _0^\bullet H^n_{s-}\textrm{d}X^n_s)$$\end{document}(Xn,∫0∙Hs-ndXsn) converges weakly to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X,\int _0^\bullet H_{s-} \textrm{d}X_s)$$\end{document}(X,∫0∙Hs-dXs) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ),d_{M_1})$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,∞),dM1) if the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn satisfy (F1)–(F3) and (AVCI), again by Proposition 2.12. This holds, e.g., if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n,X^n)\Rightarrow (H,X)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn)⇒(H,X) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{d}}[0,\infty ),d_{M_1})\times (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{d}}[0,\infty ),d_{M_1}) $$\end{document}(DRd[0,∞),dM1)×(DRd[0,∞),dM1) with H and X having no common jump discontinuities a.s., by (ii) of Proposition 2.8.

Instead of (AVCI), in the previous paragraph one can alternatively seek to verify (a) and (b) of Theorem 4.1. Correlated CTRWs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn with a finite correlation structure (i.e., there exists J\ge 1J≥1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j=0$$\end{document}cj=0 for j\ge Jj≥J) meet the requirements of Proposition 4.4, so in this case (a) and (b) of Theorem 4.1 are satisfied for integrands of the form (4.2).

Finally, we can observe the following, which links back to the discussion in Section 3.2.

Proposition 5.2

(Localised uniform integrability) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn be uncorrelated, possibly coupled, CTRWs, as given by (5.2) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0>0$$\end{document}c0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j=0$$\end{document}cj=0 for all j\ge 1j≥1. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1,2]$$\end{document}α∈(1,2], the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn are martingales and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X_n)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(Xn)n≥1 is localised uniformly integrable in the sense of Definition 3.3.

If, e.g., we only had access to earlier results on scaling limits in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology, this tells us that Corollary 3.5 applies for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1,2]$$\end{document}α∈(1,2] and so we would obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 convergence.

Explosion of integrals for correlated continuous-time random walks

An early version of (5.3) was first proved in [2] for moving averages \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn. These can be seen as special cases of correlated CTRWs with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_i \equiv 1$$\end{document}Ji≡1 for i\ge 1i≥1. [2] (and [51, Thm. 4.7.1]) showed that, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {E}}[\theta _0] = 0$$\end{document}E[θ0]=0 when 1< α < 21<α<2, or if the law of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _0$$\end{document}θ0 is symmetric when α =1α=1, then5.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X^n = \frac{1}{n^{\frac{1}{\alpha }}} \; \sum _{k=1}^{ \lfloor n \bullet \rfloor } \, \zeta _k \; \; {\mathop {{\Rightarrow } }\limits ^{{M_1}}} \; \; \Bigl (\,\sum _{j=0}^\infty c_j\Bigr )Z. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xn=1n1α∑k=1⌊n∙⌋ζk⇒M1(∑j=0∞cj)Z.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,2)$$\end{document}α∈(0,2), this cannot be strengthened to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 if both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0>0$$\end{document}c0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j>0$$\end{document}cj>0 for some j\ge 1j≥1, while the weak convergence is in fact in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j=0$$\end{document}cj=0 for all j\ge 1j≥1.

In [23], Jakubowski singled out the strict \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 convergence in (5.5) to motivate that, when studying the weak convergence of stochastic integrals, ‘for some naturally arising integrators the requirement of convergence in the usual Skorohod topology may be too strong’ (see [23, Ex. 2]). Whether or not weak continuity results for stochastic integrals actually apply to these integrators, however, is not discussed in that work. In fact, we are not aware of any later works that have addressed this point. The next example shows that, for strictly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 convergent moving averages and correlated CTRWs, we do not have weak convergence in general.

Proposition 5.3

(Exploding integrals) Consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1,2)$$\end{document}α∈(1,2), and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn be given by (5.5) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _k = c_0\theta _k + c_1 \theta _{k-1}$$\end{document}ζk=c0θk+c1θk-1, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0, c_1>0$$\end{document}c0,c1>0. One can choose centered \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _k$$\end{document}θk in such a way that there exists a sequence of pure jump càdlàg integrands \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^n$$\end{document}Hn adapted to the natural filtration generated by the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n$$\end{document}Xn and converging uniformly to zero, almost surely, for which all finite-dimensional distributions of the stochastic integrals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^\bullet H^n_{s-} \textrm{d}X^n_s$$\end{document}∫0∙Hs-ndXsn explode as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞.

From this result, we can also conclude that: for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1,2)$$\end{document}α∈(1,2), correlated CTRWs in general do not admit good decompositions (GD). Otherwise, we would have a contradiction to the conclusion of Theorem 2.6, by virtue of (5.5) and condition (ii) of Proposition 2.8. Nevertheless, we note that the weak convergence framework introduced in this paper can be extended to yield \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 continuity of stochastic integrals driven by correlated CTRWs under additional assumptions on the integrands; we pursue these extensions in [46].

Proofs of the results from section 2

We provide the proofs in the order in which the results were presented.

The key consequence of AVCI and its alternative

The central point of (AVCI) and the alternative condition given in Theorem 4.1 is the following: it allows us to start from specific stochastic integrals with respect to integrands discretised along path-dependent stopping times and then approximate these by stochastic integrals with respect to integrands discretised along deterministic times. This is a pivotal ingredient in the proof of Theorem 2.6 and is made precise by Proposition 6.3 below, after some definitions.

For a given partition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho =\{0=s_0<s_1<...\}$$\end{document}ρ={0=s0<s1<...} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_n \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}sn→∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{\rho }: \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0, \infty ) \rightarrow \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}Iρ:DRd[0,∞)→DRd[0,∞) the discretisation mapping defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ I_\rho (z) \; = \; \sum _{i=0}^{\infty } \, z(s_i) {{\textbf{1}}}_{[s_i,s_{i+1})}.$$\end{document}Iρ(z)=∑i=0∞z(si)1[si,si+1).

Since η η was chosen arbitrarily, this yields the claim. For a proof of the statement under (AVCI) we refer to [23]. □ □

Weak continuity of stochastic integrals

Proof of Proposition 2.5

The proof follows similarly to Jakubowski, Mémin, & Pagès [24, Thm. 2.1, Lem. 1.3, & Lem. 1.1], only we rely on (GD) in the end. For completeness we give the details.

Proof of Proposition 2.5

We only show the first part of the proposition. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {{F}}_{t})_{t\ge 0}$$\end{document}(Ft)t≥0 denote the natural filtration generated by X, and let G be an arbitrary simple predictable process as in [43, Ch. 2, Sec. 1]. Using a simple approximation argument for the stopping times used in this definition, it is not hard to see that we can assume them to be deterministic times. Proof

The proof proceeds as in [20, Lem. I.3.30] with the L-domination property satisfied due to the classical Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality. □ □

We can now prove Theorems 2.6 and 4.1. We only consider the case in which the pairs of integrands and integrators converge weakly on the product space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty ), \, d_{M_1})\times (\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty ), \, d_{M_1})$$\end{document}(DRd[0,∞),dM1)×(DRd[0,∞),dM1), as in (2.4). The statements on convergence together on the strong \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 space, as in (2.3), follow analogously (it will be clear from the proof where and how things should be changed).

\; {\mathbb {E}}^n \Big [ \, \big | \, \int _0^\bullet (H^n_{s-}-H^{n\, \vert \, m, \varepsilon }_{s-}) \textrm{d}M^n_s\, \big |^*_T \; \wedge \; 1 \, \Big ] \; \longrightarrow \; 0$$\end{document}lim supn→∞supm≥1En[|∫0∙(Hs-n-Hs-n|m,ε)dMsn|T∗∧1]⟶0as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0, due to the tightness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(|M^n|^*_T)_{n\ge 1}$$\end{document}(|Mn|T∗)n≥1 and the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta , \delta ,\gamma $$\end{document}β,δ,γ were arbitrary (first choose β β small enough and δ δ large enough to make the first and last term small, then increase γ γ to downsize the third term and finally let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0. Hence, there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_3\ge 1$$\end{document}m3≥1 such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\ge m_3$$\end{document}m≥m3 and all \varepsilon >0ε>0 it holds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\limsup _{n\rightarrow \infty } |(T3)| \le \theta /3$$\end{document}lim supn→∞|(T3)|≤θ/3.

(T2): In a last step, we seek to control (T2). First, fix \varepsilon ε such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< \varepsilon < \varepsilon _4$$\end{document}0<ε<ε4 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\ge \text {max}\{m_1,m_3\}$$\end{document}m≥max{m1,m3}. We recall, that according to the previous two points, it then holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\limsup _{n\rightarrow \infty } [ \, | (T1)| + |(T3)| + |(T4)| \, ] \le \theta $$\end{document}lim supn→∞[|(T1)|+|(T3)|+|(T4)|]≤θ. By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, there exists a probability space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({{\tilde{\Omega }}}, \tilde{{\mathcal {F}}}, {{\tilde{{\mathbb {P}}}}})$$\end{document}(Ω~,F~,P~) and random vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tilde{H}^n, \tilde{X}^n)$$\end{document}(H~n,X~n) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\tilde{H}},{\tilde{X}})$$\end{document}(H~,X~) on this common probability space, mapping into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )\times \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}DRd[0,∞)×DRd[0,∞), such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tilde{H}^n, \tilde{X}^n) \; Hence, there is almost sure convergence as above such that the limits do not have discontinuities at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^m_0,t^m_1,...$$\end{document}t0m,t1m,... .

For simplicity of notation, we will denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({{\tilde{\Omega }}}, \tilde{{\mathcal {F}}}, {{\tilde{{\mathbb {P}}}}})$$\end{document}(Ω~,F~,P~) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega , {\mathcal {F}}, {\mathbb {P}})$$\end{document}(Ω,F,P), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tilde{H}^n, \tilde{X}^n)$$\end{document}(H~n,X~n) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n, X^n)$$\end{document}(Hn,Xn) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\tilde{H}}, {\tilde{X}})$$\end{document}(H~,X~) by (H, X) as there is no risk of confusion at this point. Due to the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^m_0,t^m_1,...$$\end{document}t0m,t1m,... are almost surely continuity points of H, it is well known that the projection maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{t^m_i}: \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty ) \rightarrow \textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^d}[0,\infty )$$\end{document}πtim:DRd[0,∞)→DRd[0,∞) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{t^m_i}(z)=z(t^m_i)$$\end{document}πtim(z)=z(tim) are continuous. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^n_{t^m_0},..., H^n_{t^m_{k}}) \rightarrow (H_{t^m_0},..., H_{t^m_{k}})$$\end{document}(Ht0mn,...,Htkmn)→(Ht0m,...,Htkm) almost surely in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^{k}$$\end{document}Rk as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞ for all k\ge 1k≥1, and thus for all s>0s>0,almost surely with respect to the uniform topology (and therefore also in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_1$$\end{document}J1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology), since there are only finitely many \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^m_i \le s$$\end{document}tim≤s. In particular, the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{-}^{n\, \vert \, m}|_{[0,s]}$$\end{document}H-n|m|[0,s], n\ge 1n≥1, almost surely has a uniformly bounded number of discontinuities. In addition, we know that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Disc}_{\mathbb {P}}(X)=\{q>0\,: \, {\mathbb {P}}(\Delta X_q \ne 0)> 0\}$$\end{document}DiscP(X)={q>0:P(ΔXq≠0)>0} is countable and for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \notin \text {Disc}_{\mathbb {P}}(X)$$\end{document}s∉DiscP(X) it holds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^n|_{[0,s]} \rightarrow X|_{[0,s]}$$\end{document}Xn|[0,s]→X|[0,s] almost surely. Further, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{\bullet \, -}^{\, \vert \, m}$$\end{document}H∙-|m and X almost surely have no common discontinuities (as X almost surely is continuous at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^m_0,t^m_{1},...$$\end{document}t0m,t1m,...). Proposition 2.15 on simple integral convergence for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho =d_{M_1}$$\end{document}ρ=dM1 (or [28, Equations (1.12)-(1.13)] for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho =d_{J_1}$$\end{document}ρ=dJ1) therefore gives us thatfor all s>0s>0 in a co-countable subset of [0,∞ )[0,∞) almost surely on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,s], \, \rho )$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,s],ρ). By construction of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 metric for the time interval [0,∞ )[0,∞), this directly implies that we also have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( X^n ,\, \int _0^{\bullet } \, H^{n\, \vert \, m}_{ r- } \, \textrm{d}X^n_r )\rightarrow ( X, \,\int _0^{\bullet } \, H^{\, \vert \, m}_{ r- } \, \textrm{d}X_r )$$\end{document}(Xn,∫0∙Hr-n|mdXrn)→(X,∫0∙Hr-|mdXr) almost surely on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D}_{{\mathbb {R}}^{2d}}[0,\infty ), \, d_{M_1})$$\end{document}(DR2d[0,∞),dM1). Thus, by dominated convergence and the continuity of f for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}M1 topology, |(T2)| → 0|(T2)|→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞.

Consequently, for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\varepsilon <\varepsilon _4$$\end{document}0<ε<ε4 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\ge \text {max}\{m_1,m_3\}$$\end{document}m≥max{m1,m3} it holds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\limsup _{n\rightarrow \infty } [|(T1)|+ |(T2)| + |(T3)| +|(T4)| ] \le \theta $$\end{document}lim supn→∞[|(T1)|+|(T2)|+|(T3)|+|(T4)|]≤θ. As θ >0θ>0 was chosen arbitrarily, this gives us (6.3). □ □

Proof of Proposition 2.8

Proof of Proposition 2.8

Proof of Corollary 2.14

Again from uniqueness of weak limits, this yields [Z,X]=[Y,X][Z,X]=[Y,X] and therefore, as described above, [Z-Y]=0[Z-Y]=0. Finally, we observe that the process Z is a local martingale. Indeed, first choose a sequence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb{F}^n$$\end{document}Fn-stopping times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^n$$\end{document}σn such that the processes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^n_{\bullet \, \wedge \, \sigma ^n}$$\end{document}Y∙∧σnn are martingales and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^n_{\bullet \, \wedge \, \sigma ^n } \Rightarrow Z$$\end{document}Y∙∧σnn⇒Z.
